I'll be standing there, right? And I know I shouldn't be eating that fourth enchilada. I'm already full. My stomach is physically tight, practically telling me, \"Hey, buddy, we're good here, we had dinner hours ago.\" And I'm a logical guy, you know? At the warehouse, if a pallet is full, you don't keep stacking boxes on it. It'll tip over, it's a safety hazard, you just stop and get a new pallet. It's basic common sense. \n\nBut with this... what really worries me is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I literally tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago.\" I say it in my head, sometimes I even whisper it out loud to the open fridge. But my hand just keeps going back, like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nIt’s like the signal from my brain to my arm just gets cut off, like a frayed wire on one of our forklifts. I just stand there, chewing, feeling so pathetic about the whole thing. And then, while I'm still eating, I start stressing about the next day—how I'm going to feel bloated and exhausted, and how I'm gonna have to bury the empty Tupperware at the very bottom of the kitchen trash bin so my wife doesn't see it when she takes the bag out. Because she's so proud of her cooking, right? If she saw I ate three days' worth of her meal prep in twenty minutes... man, it would break her heart, or she'd just think I've lost my mind. So I'm standing there, stressing about the trash, stressing about my stomach, practically begging myself to stop, but the hand just keeps scooping. It’s completely out of my control. I just can't stop the train once it leaves the station.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's actually... it's completely different from how I normally eat. \n\nWhen we sit down for dinner, the whole family, you know, we really take our time. We sit around the table or the kitchen island, we pass the dishes around, we talk about the kids' day at school, I tell them about what happened down at the distribution center. I savor the meal. My wife, she puts a lot of love into her cooking, and I always make sure to enjoy it, to really taste the spices and everything. \n\nBut at one in the morning, standing there in the dark? Man, it's a completely different story. I'm eating so fast, doc. It's nothing at all like when I'm sitting with the family. It's almost like a race against the clock. I'm just shoveling it in, trying to get it all done and over with before anyone wakes up. I'm constantly listening for the floorboards upstairs to creak, terrified my wife is going to get up to use the bathroom or get a glass of water and catch me standing there with my hand in the Tupperware. So I just pack it away as quickly as humanly possible. \n\nAnd because I'm going at that speed, I'm barely even tasting the food. I mean, I know what I'm eating, obviously, but I'm not enjoying it. I'm not savoring the sauce or the cheese or anything. It's just going down the hatch. It reminds me of when we have a rush order at the loading dock, and the trucks are lined up all the way out to the street. You're not looking at the labels on the boxes anymore, you're not organizing them neatly, you're just throwing them onto the pallets as fast as you can to clear the backlog. That's exactly how I'm eating. Just throwing it in, barely chewing. \n\nSometimes I'll even get this sharp pain in my chest or my stomach just from how fast it's all hitting my system, probably swallowing a whole bunch of air along with it. And that just makes the guilt so much worse, you know? Because I'm standing there thinking, what is even the point of eating all my wife's wonderful leftovers if I'm not even taking the time to taste them? I'm just inhaling it in the dark like a thief in my own kitchen, rushing through it so I can hide the evidence before the sun comes up. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, doing it like that.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself continuing to eat even after you feel full, to the point where you feel physically uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly how it ends every single time, doc. It goes right back to what I was saying before about the control thing, blowing right past that normal stopping point. I'll feel that signal, you know, the one where any normal person would say, \"Alright, I'm satisfied,\" but I just keep going and going until I'm in actual physical pain. \n\nBy the time I finally force myself to stop—which, honestly, is usually just because the Tupperware is scraped completely clean—my stomach feels so incredibly tight and heavy. It's this awful, stretched-out feeling, kind of like when some of the new guys at the warehouse wrap a pallet way too tight with the industrial shrink wrap and the cardboard boxes start bowing in. That's exactly what my gut feels like. It's just this heavy, solid block sitting right in my middle. \n\nAnd the worst part is what comes after. Because once I've washed the fork and buried the container in the trash, I can't even just go back upstairs and go to sleep. I'd love to just crawl back under the covers and forget it happened, but I physically can't do it. I end up having to sneak into the living room, in the pitch dark, and I just sit there on the edge of the couch for a good while. I have to sit right on the edge, keeping myself propped up, because if I try to lie down, or even if I just bend over slightly to adjust my slippers or something, it feels so bad. It's just this intense, miserable pressure. Bending over or lying flat just makes everything push up, and it hurts, it really does. \n\nSo I just sit there on the edge of the cushions, staring at the wall, listening to the house settle, just waiting for that heavy, tight feeling to pass enough so I can finally go lie horizontally in my bed without feeling like my stomach is going to split open. It takes a long time, too. It's a pretty pathetic way to spend the middle of the night, let me tell you, just sitting there too full to even lay your head down on a pillow.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you're not actually hungry?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that’s the crazy part about all this. A lot of times I'm not even hungry. I mean, not even a little bit. 

Like I was saying earlier about my wife's cooking, we always have a proper, sit-down dinner as a family. Usually around 6:30 or 7:00. And it’s a heavy meal, you know? Meat, potatoes, rice, beans—real solid food that sticks to your ribs. I eat a full portion, sometimes I even go back for a little extra while we're all sitting around talking. By the time I help clear the plates and load the dishwasher, I am completely stuffed. I’ll go sit in my recliner to watch some TV and I’m totally satisfied. The tank is completely full. 

So, you'd think that would be it for the night, right? If you're not hungry, you don't eat. It's like down at the loading dock—if a freight truck is already loaded to maximum weight capacity, you don't try to cram another pallet in there just for the heck of it. There's no need for it, the job is done. But with me... fast forward a few hours, the house is dead quiet, everyone is asleep, and I'm not waking up because my stomach is growling. There's zero physical hunger driving it. 

But I still end up wandering downstairs, padding across the kitchen floor in my socks, and going straight for the handle. A lot of times I'm not even hungry, because I already had that big dinner with everybody, but later I still end up opening the fridge and starting again. I'll just stand there staring into it, bathed in that little yellow light, knowing full well my body doesn't need the food. But I just pull the Tupperware out anyway. It’s like my brain completely ignores the fact that I’m not hungry and just forces me to eat regardless. It really makes no logical sense when you sit down and actually think about it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.81, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doc. Thanks for seeing me. It's, uh... it's a bit of a long story, I guess. I work down at the regional distribution center—shipping, receiving, inventory, that whole mess. Been doing it for years, pretty much since high school. It's a lot of being on your feet, counting pallets, making sure the manifests match up, dealing with the drivers. So by the time I get home, I'm just completely drained. \n\nBut that's not why I'm here, not exactly. It's more about what happens after all that, late at night. The house is quiet, everybody is asleep, and I'll head into the kitchen. I'll just start picking at things, you know? A little of this, a little from the pantry, maybe some leftovers. But what really worries me, and what finally got me to actually make this appointment and come in, is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago. You're done.\" I literally say it in my head. But my hand... my hand just keeps going back for more. It's like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nI'll just be standing there in the glow of the fridge or the pantry light, and my hand just has a mind of its own, grabbing more even when I know I should stop. It's incredibly frustrating because at work, I'm the one controlling the inventory, right? I'm in charge of what goes in and out. But in my own kitchen, it's like I lose all say in the matter. I just want to know why I can't just shut the cabinet door and walk away.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes in the kitchen, do you ever make yourself sick, exercise a lot, fast, or do anything else to try to compensate for what you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some folks do, you see it on TV or whatever, but that's not me. After it happens, after I finally manage to close the fridge or put the boxes away, I just... I usually just go straight to bed. Or I try to, anyway. I'll lay there and my stomach will be in knots, just so uncomfortable because I've eaten way too much, you know? And I'll think to myself, \"Why did you do that again, Santiago? You know you're going to feel like garbage tomorrow.\" \n\nAnd I do. I wake up feeling heavy, sluggish, like I'm dragging a ton of bricks with me to the distribution center. My joints ache more, my energy is just shot. I'll be staring at a manifest, trying to reconcile the numbers, and my brain is just foggy. But as far as trying to undo it? Throwing up or starving myself the next day? No, I don't do that. I just kind of carry it with me, physically and mentally. It's like a punishment I just accept, I guess. I just go to work, deal with the drivers, count the pallets, and try to ignore how awful I feel until the cycle starts all over again that night.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen, and do they seem to follow any kind of pattern or happen more often under certain circumstances?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I'd say for the past several months, it's been pretty consistent. It's not every single night, thank God, but it's happening most weeks, at least once a week, sometimes maybe twice if things get really out of hand. \n\nAs for a pattern, yeah, I guess there is one. It almost always happens on a night when there are plenty of leftovers sitting in the fridge. You know, my wife, she loves to cook, bless her heart. She makes these big batches of arroz con pollo or enchiladas, thinking she's helping out so we have lunches for the week. And they're great, they really are. But then they're just sitting there, in those little plastic containers, stacked up on the middle shelf. \n\nAnd the other thing is, it always, always happens after everyone else has gone to bed. The kids are asleep, my wife is asleep, the TV is off. The whole house gets this heavy kind of quiet. And I'll be sitting in my recliner, maybe trying to unwind from the day, thinking about the pallets I gotta count tomorrow, and I'll hear the hum of the refrigerator. It's like it's calling to me, I swear. I'll just walk in there, open the door, and see all those containers, and before I know it, I'm eating cold rice straight out of the Tupperware. It's just that combination, you know? The quiet house, the food just sitting there waiting, and me being the only one awake to know what's happening. It's like the perfect storm for me to just lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes cause you significant distress, or do they interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's a big question. Distress? Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it eats at me, no pun intended. I lay there at night and I just feel this heavy, sinking guilt in my chest, thinking about what I just did. And the next morning, I'm exhausted, like I told you, just dragging myself around. \n\nBut as far as interfering with my daily life... it's tricky. I mean, I'm still getting to my shipping and receiving shifts on time. I clock in, I check the manifests, I deal with the drivers, I count the pallets. I'm doing my job, you know? I haven't missed a day of work because of this. I'm still providing for my family, still paying the bills. On paper, if you just looked at my schedule, you'd think everything was perfectly fine. Santiago goes to work, Santiago comes home. \n\nBut... but it is changing things at home, in ways that really bother me. See, my wife and kids, sometimes they'll be up a little later, maybe watching a movie in the living room or sitting around the kitchen island having a snack, just talking and laughing. And I used to love that. I used to love just sitting there with them, hearing about their day. But lately... lately I've started avoiding that late family time, especially if they're in or near the kitchen. \n\nI'll just make an excuse, say I'm tired from the distribution center—which isn't a total lie, I am tired—and I'll just head to the bedroom or go sit out in the garage. And the real reason I do it is because I'm just so worried they're going to notice how uneasy I am around the food. If there's a bowl of chips out, or if I see those leftover containers in the fridge when someone opens it to get a drink... I get this anxious, tight feeling. I don't want them to see me staring at it, or see me looking nervous, or ask me why I'm acting weird. So I just remove myself from the situation entirely. I'm missing out on time with my own family in my own house because I'm scared of how I'll act around the food, or what they might see in my face. So yeah, I guess it is interfering, just... just not with my job, but with the stuff that actually matters more.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing in front of the fridge or pantry during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself even though you know you should?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's exactly it. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, to be honest with you. It's not just about eating the leftovers, it's about... it's about what happens in my head while I'm doing it. \n\nI'll be standing there, right? The fridge door is open, the little light is on, casting that glow on the kitchen floor. I've got a container of enchiladas open, maybe just a fork, maybe I'm just using my fingers if I'm being completely honest, which I hate admitting. And I'll take a bite, and it tastes good, sure. But then, almost immediately, this voice in my head—my own voice, you know?—says, \"Okay, Santiago, that's enough. You had a taste. Put the lid back on. Close the fridge. Go back to your chair or go to bed.\" I hear it loud and clear. I know I should stop. I know exactly how I'm going to feel the next morning at the distribution center, dragging my feet, feeling that heavy fog while I'm trying to check the invoices. \n\nBut... but it's like my body doesn't listen to my brain anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating feeling. I'll be telling myself to stop, practically begging myself to just put the plastic lid back on the Tupperware, but my hand... my hand just keeps going back down into the container. It's like I don't even get to make the decision anymore. It's like my arm has a mind of its own, just scoop, lift, chew, swallow. Scoop, lift, chew, swallow. Over and over. \n\nAnd I'm just standing there, watching myself do it, feeling so helpless. I'll think, \"Just step back, Santiago, just take one step back,\" but my feet stay planted right there on the linoleum. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body until the container is empty, or until I'm so uncomfortably full that I physically can't take another bite. That loss of control, doc, it's... it's really messing with my head. Because I'm a guy who likes to be in control. I manage inventory, I keep things organized, I make sure the trucks are loaded right. But standing in front of my own refrigerator at midnight? I have absolutely zero control. It just takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, would you say that you feel completely unable to control how much you eat once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, doc. Like I was just trying to say a second ago, that's the whole thing in a nutshell. It's a complete and total lack of control. \n\nI mean, it's not just a little slip-up where you eat an extra cookie and think, \"Oh well, I shouldn't have done that.\" It's... it's like a complete hijacking of my own body. I'll be standing there, right? And I know I shouldn't be eating that fourth enchilada. I'm already full. My stomach is physically tight, practically telling me, \"Hey, buddy, we're good here, we had dinner hours ago.\" And I'm a logical guy, you know? At the warehouse, if a pallet is full, you don't keep stacking boxes on it. It'll tip over, it's a safety hazard, you just stop and get a new pallet. It's basic common sense. \n\nBut with this... what really worries me is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I literally tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago.\" I say it in my head, sometimes I even whisper it out loud to the open fridge. But my hand just keeps going back, like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nIt’s like the signal from my brain to my arm just gets cut off, like a frayed wire on one of our forklifts. I just stand there, chewing, feeling so pathetic about the whole thing. And then, while I'm still eating, I start stressing about the next day—how I'm going to feel bloated and exhausted, and how I'm gonna have to bury the empty Tupperware at the very bottom of the kitchen trash bin so my wife doesn't see it when she takes the bag out. Because she's so proud of her cooking, right? If she saw I ate three days' worth of her meal prep in twenty minutes... man, it would break her heart, or she'd just think I've lost my mind. So I'm standing there, stressing about the trash, stressing about my stomach, practically begging myself to stop, but the hand just keeps scooping. It’s completely out of my control. I just can't stop the train once it leaves the station.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's actually... it's completely different from how I normally eat. \n\nWhen we sit down for dinner, the whole family, you know, we really take our time. We sit around the table or the kitchen island, we pass the dishes around, we talk about the kids' day at school, I tell them about what happened down at the distribution center. I savor the meal. My wife, she puts a lot of love into her cooking, and I always make sure to enjoy it, to really taste the spices and everything. \n\nBut at one in the morning, standing there in the dark? Man, it's a completely different story. I'm eating so fast, doc. It's nothing at all like when I'm sitting with the family. It's almost like a race against the clock. I'm just shoveling it in, trying to get it all done and over with before anyone wakes up. I'm constantly listening for the floorboards upstairs to creak, terrified my wife is going to get up to use the bathroom or get a glass of water and catch me standing there with my hand in the Tupperware. So I just pack it away as quickly as humanly possible. \n\nAnd because I'm going at that speed, I'm barely even tasting the food. I mean, I know what I'm eating, obviously, but I'm not enjoying it. I'm not savoring the sauce or the cheese or anything. It's just going down the hatch. It reminds me of when we have a rush order at the loading dock, and the trucks are lined up all the way out to the street. You're not looking at the labels on the boxes anymore, you're not organizing them neatly, you're just throwing them onto the pallets as fast as you can to clear the backlog. That's exactly how I'm eating. Just throwing it in, barely chewing. \n\nSometimes I'll even get this sharp pain in my chest or my stomach just from how fast it's all hitting my system, probably swallowing a whole bunch of air along with it. And that just makes the guilt so much worse, you know? Because I'm standing there thinking, what is even the point of eating all my wife's wonderful leftovers if I'm not even taking the time to taste them? I'm just inhaling it in the dark like a thief in my own kitchen, rushing through it so I can hide the evidence before the sun comes up. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, doing it like that.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself continuing to eat even after you feel full, to the point where you feel physically uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly how it ends every single time, doc. It goes right back to what I was saying before about the control thing, blowing right past that normal stopping point. I'll feel that signal, you know, the one where any normal person would say, \"Alright, I'm satisfied,\" but I just keep going and going until I'm in actual physical pain. \n\nBy the time I finally force myself to stop—which, honestly, is usually just because the Tupperware is scraped completely clean—my stomach feels so incredibly tight and heavy. It's this awful, stretched-out feeling, kind of like when some of the new guys at the warehouse wrap a pallet way too tight with the industrial shrink wrap and the cardboard boxes start bowing in. That's exactly what my gut feels like. It's just this heavy, solid block sitting right in my middle. \n\nAnd the worst part is what comes after. Because once I've washed the fork and buried the container in the trash, I can't even just go back upstairs and go to sleep. I'd love to just crawl back under the covers and forget it happened, but I physically can't do it. I end up having to sneak into the living room, in the pitch dark, and I just sit there on the edge of the couch for a good while. I have to sit right on the edge, keeping myself propped up, because if I try to lie down, or even if I just bend over slightly to adjust my slippers or something, it feels so bad. It's just this intense, miserable pressure. Bending over or lying flat just makes everything push up, and it hurts, it really does. \n\nSo I just sit there on the edge of the cushions, staring at the wall, listening to the house settle, just waiting for that heavy, tight feeling to pass enough so I can finally go lie horizontally in my bed without feeling like my stomach is going to split open. It takes a long time, too. It's a pretty pathetic way to spend the middle of the night, let me tell you, just sitting there too full to even lay your head down on a pillow.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you're not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s the crazy part about all this. A lot of times I'm not even hungry. I mean, not even a little bit. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about my wife's cooking, we always have a proper, sit-down dinner as a family. Usually around 6:30 or 7:00. And it’s a heavy meal, you know? Meat, potatoes, rice, beans—real solid food that sticks to your ribs. I eat a full portion, sometimes I even go back for a little extra while we're all sitting around talking. By the time I help clear the plates and load the dishwasher, I am completely stuffed. I’ll go sit in my recliner to watch some TV and I’m totally satisfied. The tank is completely full. \n\nSo, you'd think that would be it for the night, right? If you're not hungry, you don't eat. It's like down at the loading dock—if a freight truck is already loaded to maximum weight capacity, you don't try to cram another pallet in there just for the heck of it. There's no need for it, the job is done. But with me... fast forward a few hours, the house is dead quiet, everyone is asleep, and I'm not waking up because my stomach is growling. There's zero physical hunger driving it. \n\nBut I still end up wandering downstairs, padding across the kitchen floor in my socks, and going straight for the handle. A lot of times I'm not even hungry, because I already had that big dinner with everybody, but later I still end up opening the fridge and starting again. I'll just stand there staring into it, bathed in that little yellow light, knowing full well my body doesn't need the food. But I just pull the Tupperware out anyway. It’s like my brain completely ignores the fact that I’m not hungry and just forces me to eat regardless. It really makes no logical sense when you sit down and actually think about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, I do. It's actually... it's completely different from how I normally eat. \n\nWhen we sit down for dinner, the whole family, you know, we really take our time. We sit around the table or the kitchen island, we pass the dishes around, we talk about the kids' day at school, I tell them about what happened down at the distribution center. I savor the meal. My wife, she puts a lot of love into her cooking, and I always make sure to enjoy it, to really taste the spices and everything. \n\nBut at one in the morning, standing there in the dark? Man, it's a completely different story. I'm eating so fast, doc. It's nothing at all like when I'm sitting with the family. It's almost like a race against the clock. I'm just shoveling it in, trying to get it all done and over with before anyone wakes up. I'm constantly listening for the floorboards upstairs to creak, terrified my wife is going to get up to use the bathroom or get a glass of water and catch me standing there with my hand in the Tupperware. So I just pack it away as quickly as humanly possible. \n\nAnd because I'm going at that speed, I'm barely even tasting the food. I mean, I know what I'm eating, obviously, but I'm not enjoying it. I'm not savoring the sauce or the cheese or anything. It's just going down the hatch. It reminds me of when we have a rush order at the loading dock, and the trucks are lined up all the way out to the street. You're not looking at the labels on the boxes anymore, you're not organizing them neatly, you're just throwing them onto the pallets as fast as you can to clear the backlog. That's exactly how I'm eating. Just throwing it in, barely chewing. \n\nSometimes I'll even get this sharp pain in my chest or my stomach just from how fast it's all hitting my system, probably swallowing a whole bunch of air along with it. And that just makes the guilt so much worse, you know? Because I'm standing there thinking, what is even the point of eating all my wife's wonderful leftovers if I'm not even taking the time to taste them? It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, doing it like that.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself continuing to eat even after you feel full, to the point where you feel physically uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's this awful, stretched-out feeling, kind of like when some of the new guys at the warehouse wrap a pallet way too tight with the industrial shrink wrap and the cardboard boxes start bowing in. That's exactly what my gut feels like. It's just this heavy, solid block sitting right in my middle. \n\nAnd the worst part is what comes after. It's just this intense, miserable pressure. Bending over or lying flat just makes everything push up, and it hurts, it really does. \n\nSo I just sit there on the edge of the cushions, staring at the wall, listening to the house settle, just waiting for that heavy, tight feeling to pass enough so I can finally go lie horizontally in my bed without feeling like my stomach is going to split open. It takes a long time, too. It's a pretty pathetic way to spend the middle of the night, let me tell you, just sitting there too full to even lay your head down on a pillow.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you're not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s the crazy part about all this. I eat a full portion, sometimes I even go back for a little extra while we're all sitting around talking. By the time I help clear the plates and load the dishwasher, I am completely stuffed. I’ll go sit in my recliner to watch some TV and I’m totally satisfied. The tank is completely full. \n\nSo, you'd think that would be it for the night, right? If you're not hungry, you don't eat. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, a hundred percent, doc. The embarrassment is... well, it's the whole reason I'm doing it at one in the morning in the dark, right? I mean, if I wasn't ashamed of it, I'd just eat during the day. But I really don't like anyone seeing how much I put away. It's just an unbelievable amount of food, and if anyone actually saw the sheer volume of it, I don't know how I'd even look them in the eye the next morning. 

It's like when we do our quarterly inventory audits at the warehouse and you find a massive discrepancy on the ledger—you just don't want anyone to see it because it looks terrible, it looks totally out of control. That's exactly how I feel about the food. So I wait until my aunt—she lives with us now, she's staying in the downstairs guest room right off the kitchen hallway—and my wife and the rest of the house are completely asleep. I have to wait until I'm absolutely sure they're out cold. I'll literally lay there in bed just listening to the house, checking the clock, waiting for everything to settle down and get dead quiet. 

And then I creep down there and I keep it quiet in the kitchen. I mean, I'm talking total stealth mode. I know exactly which floorboards squeak, so I step over them. I open the fridge door super slow so the magnetic rubber seal doesn't make that loud popping noise... you know the one I'm talking about? And I don't even turn on the overhead light, just rely on the glow from the fridge. Because if my aunt woke up to get a glass of water, or if my wife came downstairs and saw me just tearing through half a roast or a whole family-sized side dish by myself... I'd be mortified. Just absolutely mortified. I couldn't handle the look on their faces. So yeah, the secrecy is all about the shame of it. I just can't let them see me like that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.81, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doc. Thanks for seeing me. It's, uh... it's a bit of a long story, I guess. I work down at the regional distribution center—shipping, receiving, inventory, that whole mess. Been doing it for years, pretty much since high school. It's a lot of being on your feet, counting pallets, making sure the manifests match up, dealing with the drivers. So by the time I get home, I'm just completely drained. \n\nBut that's not why I'm here, not exactly. It's more about what happens after all that, late at night. The house is quiet, everybody is asleep, and I'll head into the kitchen. I'll just start picking at things, you know? A little of this, a little from the pantry, maybe some leftovers. But what really worries me, and what finally got me to actually make this appointment and come in, is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago. You're done.\" I literally say it in my head. But my hand... my hand just keeps going back for more. It's like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nI'll just be standing there in the glow of the fridge or the pantry light, and my hand just has a mind of its own, grabbing more even when I know I should stop. It's incredibly frustrating because at work, I'm the one controlling the inventory, right? I'm in charge of what goes in and out. But in my own kitchen, it's like I lose all say in the matter. I just want to know why I can't just shut the cabinet door and walk away.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes in the kitchen, do you ever make yourself sick, exercise a lot, fast, or do anything else to try to compensate for what you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some folks do, you see it on TV or whatever, but that's not me. After it happens, after I finally manage to close the fridge or put the boxes away, I just... I usually just go straight to bed. Or I try to, anyway. I'll lay there and my stomach will be in knots, just so uncomfortable because I've eaten way too much, you know? And I'll think to myself, \"Why did you do that again, Santiago? You know you're going to feel like garbage tomorrow.\" \n\nAnd I do. I wake up feeling heavy, sluggish, like I'm dragging a ton of bricks with me to the distribution center. My joints ache more, my energy is just shot. I'll be staring at a manifest, trying to reconcile the numbers, and my brain is just foggy. But as far as trying to undo it? Throwing up or starving myself the next day? No, I don't do that. I just kind of carry it with me, physically and mentally. It's like a punishment I just accept, I guess. I just go to work, deal with the drivers, count the pallets, and try to ignore how awful I feel until the cycle starts all over again that night.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen, and do they seem to follow any kind of pattern or happen more often under certain circumstances?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I'd say for the past several months, it's been pretty consistent. It's not every single night, thank God, but it's happening most weeks, at least once a week, sometimes maybe twice if things get really out of hand. \n\nAs for a pattern, yeah, I guess there is one. It almost always happens on a night when there are plenty of leftovers sitting in the fridge. You know, my wife, she loves to cook, bless her heart. She makes these big batches of arroz con pollo or enchiladas, thinking she's helping out so we have lunches for the week. And they're great, they really are. But then they're just sitting there, in those little plastic containers, stacked up on the middle shelf. \n\nAnd the other thing is, it always, always happens after everyone else has gone to bed. The kids are asleep, my wife is asleep, the TV is off. The whole house gets this heavy kind of quiet. And I'll be sitting in my recliner, maybe trying to unwind from the day, thinking about the pallets I gotta count tomorrow, and I'll hear the hum of the refrigerator. It's like it's calling to me, I swear. I'll just walk in there, open the door, and see all those containers, and before I know it, I'm eating cold rice straight out of the Tupperware. It's just that combination, you know? The quiet house, the food just sitting there waiting, and me being the only one awake to know what's happening. It's like the perfect storm for me to just lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes cause you significant distress, or do they interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's a big question. Distress? Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it eats at me, no pun intended. I lay there at night and I just feel this heavy, sinking guilt in my chest, thinking about what I just did. And the next morning, I'm exhausted, like I told you, just dragging myself around. \n\nBut as far as interfering with my daily life... it's tricky. I mean, I'm still getting to my shipping and receiving shifts on time. I clock in, I check the manifests, I deal with the drivers, I count the pallets. I'm doing my job, you know? I haven't missed a day of work because of this. I'm still providing for my family, still paying the bills. On paper, if you just looked at my schedule, you'd think everything was perfectly fine. Santiago goes to work, Santiago comes home. \n\nBut... but it is changing things at home, in ways that really bother me. See, my wife and kids, sometimes they'll be up a little later, maybe watching a movie in the living room or sitting around the kitchen island having a snack, just talking and laughing. And I used to love that. I used to love just sitting there with them, hearing about their day. But lately... lately I've started avoiding that late family time, especially if they're in or near the kitchen. \n\nI'll just make an excuse, say I'm tired from the distribution center—which isn't a total lie, I am tired—and I'll just head to the bedroom or go sit out in the garage. And the real reason I do it is because I'm just so worried they're going to notice how uneasy I am around the food. If there's a bowl of chips out, or if I see those leftover containers in the fridge when someone opens it to get a drink... I get this anxious, tight feeling. I don't want them to see me staring at it, or see me looking nervous, or ask me why I'm acting weird. So I just remove myself from the situation entirely. I'm missing out on time with my own family in my own house because I'm scared of how I'll act around the food, or what they might see in my face. So yeah, I guess it is interfering, just... just not with my job, but with the stuff that actually matters more.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing in front of the fridge or pantry during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself even though you know you should?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's exactly it. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, to be honest with you. It's not just about eating the leftovers, it's about... it's about what happens in my head while I'm doing it. \n\nI'll be standing there, right? The fridge door is open, the little light is on, casting that glow on the kitchen floor. I've got a container of enchiladas open, maybe just a fork, maybe I'm just using my fingers if I'm being completely honest, which I hate admitting. And I'll take a bite, and it tastes good, sure. But then, almost immediately, this voice in my head—my own voice, you know?—says, \"Okay, Santiago, that's enough. You had a taste. Put the lid back on. Close the fridge. Go back to your chair or go to bed.\" I hear it loud and clear. I know I should stop. I know exactly how I'm going to feel the next morning at the distribution center, dragging my feet, feeling that heavy fog while I'm trying to check the invoices. \n\nBut... but it's like my body doesn't listen to my brain anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating feeling. I'll be telling myself to stop, practically begging myself to just put the plastic lid back on the Tupperware, but my hand... my hand just keeps going back down into the container. It's like I don't even get to make the decision anymore. It's like my arm has a mind of its own, just scoop, lift, chew, swallow. Scoop, lift, chew, swallow. Over and over. \n\nAnd I'm just standing there, watching myself do it, feeling so helpless. I'll think, \"Just step back, Santiago, just take one step back,\" but my feet stay planted right there on the linoleum. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body until the container is empty, or until I'm so uncomfortably full that I physically can't take another bite. That loss of control, doc, it's... it's really messing with my head. Because I'm a guy who likes to be in control. I manage inventory, I keep things organized, I make sure the trucks are loaded right. But standing in front of my own refrigerator at midnight? I have absolutely zero control. It just takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, would you say that you feel completely unable to control how much you eat once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, doc. Like I was just trying to say a second ago, that's the whole thing in a nutshell. It's a complete and total lack of control. \n\nI mean, it's not just a little slip-up where you eat an extra cookie and think, \"Oh well, I shouldn't have done that.\" It's... it's like a complete hijacking of my own body. I'll be standing there, right? And I know I shouldn't be eating that fourth enchilada. I'm already full. My stomach is physically tight, practically telling me, \"Hey, buddy, we're good here, we had dinner hours ago.\" And I'm a logical guy, you know? At the warehouse, if a pallet is full, you don't keep stacking boxes on it. It'll tip over, it's a safety hazard, you just stop and get a new pallet. It's basic common sense. \n\nBut with this... what really worries me is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I literally tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago.\" I say it in my head, sometimes I even whisper it out loud to the open fridge. But my hand just keeps going back, like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nIt’s like the signal from my brain to my arm just gets cut off, like a frayed wire on one of our forklifts. I just stand there, chewing, feeling so pathetic about the whole thing. And then, while I'm still eating, I start stressing about the next day—how I'm going to feel bloated and exhausted, and how I'm gonna have to bury the empty Tupperware at the very bottom of the kitchen trash bin so my wife doesn't see it when she takes the bag out. Because she's so proud of her cooking, right? If she saw I ate three days' worth of her meal prep in twenty minutes... man, it would break her heart, or she'd just think I've lost my mind. So I'm standing there, stressing about the trash, stressing about my stomach, practically begging myself to stop, but the hand just keeps scooping. It’s completely out of my control. I just can't stop the train once it leaves the station.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's actually... it's completely different from how I normally eat. \n\nWhen we sit down for dinner, the whole family, you know, we really take our time. We sit around the table or the kitchen island, we pass the dishes around, we talk about the kids' day at school, I tell them about what happened down at the distribution center. I savor the meal. My wife, she puts a lot of love into her cooking, and I always make sure to enjoy it, to really taste the spices and everything. \n\nBut at one in the morning, standing there in the dark? Man, it's a completely different story. I'm eating so fast, doc. It's nothing at all like when I'm sitting with the family. It's almost like a race against the clock. I'm just shoveling it in, trying to get it all done and over with before anyone wakes up. I'm constantly listening for the floorboards upstairs to creak, terrified my wife is going to get up to use the bathroom or get a glass of water and catch me standing there with my hand in the Tupperware. So I just pack it away as quickly as humanly possible. \n\nAnd because I'm going at that speed, I'm barely even tasting the food. I mean, I know what I'm eating, obviously, but I'm not enjoying it. I'm not savoring the sauce or the cheese or anything. It's just going down the hatch. It reminds me of when we have a rush order at the loading dock, and the trucks are lined up all the way out to the street. You're not looking at the labels on the boxes anymore, you're not organizing them neatly, you're just throwing them onto the pallets as fast as you can to clear the backlog. That's exactly how I'm eating. Just throwing it in, barely chewing. \n\nSometimes I'll even get this sharp pain in my chest or my stomach just from how fast it's all hitting my system, probably swallowing a whole bunch of air along with it. And that just makes the guilt so much worse, you know? Because I'm standing there thinking, what is even the point of eating all my wife's wonderful leftovers if I'm not even taking the time to taste them? I'm just inhaling it in the dark like a thief in my own kitchen, rushing through it so I can hide the evidence before the sun comes up. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, doing it like that.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself continuing to eat even after you feel full, to the point where you feel physically uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly how it ends every single time, doc. It goes right back to what I was saying before about the control thing, blowing right past that normal stopping point. I'll feel that signal, you know, the one where any normal person would say, \"Alright, I'm satisfied,\" but I just keep going and going until I'm in actual physical pain. \n\nBy the time I finally force myself to stop—which, honestly, is usually just because the Tupperware is scraped completely clean—my stomach feels so incredibly tight and heavy. It's this awful, stretched-out feeling, kind of like when some of the new guys at the warehouse wrap a pallet way too tight with the industrial shrink wrap and the cardboard boxes start bowing in. That's exactly what my gut feels like. It's just this heavy, solid block sitting right in my middle. \n\nAnd the worst part is what comes after. Because once I've washed the fork and buried the container in the trash, I can't even just go back upstairs and go to sleep. I'd love to just crawl back under the covers and forget it happened, but I physically can't do it. I end up having to sneak into the living room, in the pitch dark, and I just sit there on the edge of the couch for a good while. I have to sit right on the edge, keeping myself propped up, because if I try to lie down, or even if I just bend over slightly to adjust my slippers or something, it feels so bad. It's just this intense, miserable pressure. Bending over or lying flat just makes everything push up, and it hurts, it really does. \n\nSo I just sit there on the edge of the cushions, staring at the wall, listening to the house settle, just waiting for that heavy, tight feeling to pass enough so I can finally go lie horizontally in my bed without feeling like my stomach is going to split open. It takes a long time, too. It's a pretty pathetic way to spend the middle of the night, let me tell you, just sitting there too full to even lay your head down on a pillow.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you're not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s the crazy part about all this. A lot of times I'm not even hungry. I mean, not even a little bit. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about my wife's cooking, we always have a proper, sit-down dinner as a family. Usually around 6:30 or 7:00. And it’s a heavy meal, you know? Meat, potatoes, rice, beans—real solid food that sticks to your ribs. I eat a full portion, sometimes I even go back for a little extra while we're all sitting around talking. By the time I help clear the plates and load the dishwasher, I am completely stuffed. I’ll go sit in my recliner to watch some TV and I’m totally satisfied. The tank is completely full. \n\nSo, you'd think that would be it for the night, right? If you're not hungry, you don't eat. It's like down at the loading dock—if a freight truck is already loaded to maximum weight capacity, you don't try to cram another pallet in there just for the heck of it. There's no need for it, the job is done. But with me... fast forward a few hours, the house is dead quiet, everyone is asleep, and I'm not waking up because my stomach is growling. There's zero physical hunger driving it. \n\nBut I still end up wandering downstairs, padding across the kitchen floor in my socks, and going straight for the handle. A lot of times I'm not even hungry, because I already had that big dinner with everybody, but later I still end up opening the fridge and starting again. I'll just stand there staring into it, bathed in that little yellow light, knowing full well my body doesn't need the food. But I just pull the Tupperware out anyway. It’s like my brain completely ignores the fact that I’m not hungry and just forces me to eat regardless. It really makes no logical sense when you sit down and actually think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes in secret because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, a hundred percent, doc. The embarrassment is... well, it's the whole reason I'm doing it at one in the morning in the dark, right? I mean, if I wasn't ashamed of it, I'd just eat during the day. But I really don't like anyone seeing how much I put away. It's just an unbelievable amount of food, and if anyone actually saw the sheer volume of it, I don't know how I'd even look them in the eye the next morning. \n\nIt's like when we do our quarterly inventory audits at the warehouse and you find a massive discrepancy on the ledger—you just don't want anyone to see it because it looks terrible, it looks totally out of control. That's exactly how I feel about the food. So I wait until my aunt—she lives with us now, she's staying in the downstairs guest room right off the kitchen hallway—and my wife and the rest of the house are completely asleep. I have to wait until I'm absolutely sure they're out cold. I'll literally lay there in bed just listening to the house, checking the clock, waiting for everything to settle down and get dead quiet. \n\nAnd then I creep down there and I keep it quiet in the kitchen. I mean, I'm talking total stealth mode. I know exactly which floorboards squeak, so I step over them. I open the fridge door super slow so the magnetic rubber seal doesn't make that loud popping noise... you know the one I'm talking about? And I don't even turn on the overhead light, just rely on the glow from the fridge. Because if my aunt woke up to get a glass of water, or if my wife came downstairs and saw me just tearing through half a roast or a whole family-sized side dish by myself... I'd be mortified. Just absolutely mortified. I couldn't handle the look on their faces. So yeah, the secrecy is all about the shame of it. I just can't let them see me like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s like my brain completely ignores the fact that I’m not hungry and just forces me to eat regardless. I have to wait until I'm absolutely sure they're out cold. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, doc, I have actually thought about that myself. You always hear about people doing that, right? Like eating their feelings or whatever they call it. But honestly, no. I really haven't noticed any kind of connection like that at all. It doesn't seem to matter if I am stressed or happy or just... neutral. 

Take my job, for instance. Shipping and receiving can be an absolute madhouse sometimes. Especially around the end of the month when we are trying to push all the outgoing freight out the door to make the numbers look good for corporate. Just last Thursday we had three delivery trucks show up at the exact same time, all of them needing to be unloaded right that second, and the battery died on our main forklift. I was running around with the manual pallet jack, sweating, frustrated, arguing with the drivers... I came home completely wound up, just exhausted and annoyed. But that night? Nothing. I slept like a rock. I didn't even think about going to the kitchen. 

But then, let us look at a Sunday. A totally normal, lazy Sunday. I am talking about just sitting in the yard, maybe doing a little weeding, watching a ballgame on the TV. Zero stress. Not a care in the world. I am feeling perfectly fine, in a great mood, having a laugh with my wife over dinner. We go to sleep, and sure enough, one thirty in the morning rolls around, and I am sneaking down the stairs doing exactly what I told you about, eating until my stomach hurts. So it really does not line up with being upset or down in the dumps. 

It is frustrating, honestly, because if it was just stress, then maybe I could figure it out. I could tell myself that I had a rough shift at the warehouse and I need to be careful tonight. I could mentally prepare for it. Or I could take up jogging or something to burn off the stress. But because it just happens whenever, on good days, bad days, boring days, it feels even more out of nowhere. I have tried to track it mentally. I will ask myself if I was mad that day, or if I was sad, and the answer is almost always no. I am just a regular guy going about my week. So yeah, the emotional thing, I really do not think that is what is driving the bus here. It is just its own separate thing, completely disconnected from whatever happened during the hours I was awake.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.81, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi doc. Thanks for seeing me. It's, uh... it's a bit of a long story, I guess. I work down at the regional distribution center—shipping, receiving, inventory, that whole mess. Been doing it for years, pretty much since high school. It's a lot of being on your feet, counting pallets, making sure the manifests match up, dealing with the drivers. So by the time I get home, I'm just completely drained. \n\nBut that's not why I'm here, not exactly. It's more about what happens after all that, late at night. The house is quiet, everybody is asleep, and I'll head into the kitchen. I'll just start picking at things, you know? A little of this, a little from the pantry, maybe some leftovers. But what really worries me, and what finally got me to actually make this appointment and come in, is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago. You're done.\" I literally say it in my head. But my hand... my hand just keeps going back for more. It's like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nI'll just be standing there in the glow of the fridge or the pantry light, and my hand just has a mind of its own, grabbing more even when I know I should stop. It's incredibly frustrating because at work, I'm the one controlling the inventory, right? I'm in charge of what goes in and out. But in my own kitchen, it's like I lose all say in the matter. I just want to know why I can't just shut the cabinet door and walk away.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes in the kitchen, do you ever make yourself sick, exercise a lot, fast, or do anything else to try to compensate for what you've eaten?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do anything like that. I mean, I know some folks do, you see it on TV or whatever, but that's not me. After it happens, after I finally manage to close the fridge or put the boxes away, I just... I usually just go straight to bed. Or I try to, anyway. I'll lay there and my stomach will be in knots, just so uncomfortable because I've eaten way too much, you know? And I'll think to myself, \"Why did you do that again, Santiago? You know you're going to feel like garbage tomorrow.\" \n\nAnd I do. I wake up feeling heavy, sluggish, like I'm dragging a ton of bricks with me to the distribution center. My joints ache more, my energy is just shot. I'll be staring at a manifest, trying to reconcile the numbers, and my brain is just foggy. But as far as trying to undo it? Throwing up or starving myself the next day? No, I don't do that. I just kind of carry it with me, physically and mentally. It's like a punishment I just accept, I guess. I just go to work, deal with the drivers, count the pallets, and try to ignore how awful I feel until the cycle starts all over again that night.\n\nDoctor: How often do these late-night eating episodes happen, and do they seem to follow any kind of pattern or happen more often under certain circumstances?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, it's... it's been going on for a while now. I'd say for the past several months, it's been pretty consistent. It's not every single night, thank God, but it's happening most weeks, at least once a week, sometimes maybe twice if things get really out of hand. \n\nAs for a pattern, yeah, I guess there is one. It almost always happens on a night when there are plenty of leftovers sitting in the fridge. You know, my wife, she loves to cook, bless her heart. She makes these big batches of arroz con pollo or enchiladas, thinking she's helping out so we have lunches for the week. And they're great, they really are. But then they're just sitting there, in those little plastic containers, stacked up on the middle shelf. \n\nAnd the other thing is, it always, always happens after everyone else has gone to bed. The kids are asleep, my wife is asleep, the TV is off. The whole house gets this heavy kind of quiet. And I'll be sitting in my recliner, maybe trying to unwind from the day, thinking about the pallets I gotta count tomorrow, and I'll hear the hum of the refrigerator. It's like it's calling to me, I swear. I'll just walk in there, open the door, and see all those containers, and before I know it, I'm eating cold rice straight out of the Tupperware. It's just that combination, you know? The quiet house, the food just sitting there waiting, and me being the only one awake to know what's happening. It's like the perfect storm for me to just lose control.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes cause you significant distress, or do they interfere with your daily life in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's... that's a big question. Distress? Yeah, absolutely. I mean, it eats at me, no pun intended. I lay there at night and I just feel this heavy, sinking guilt in my chest, thinking about what I just did. And the next morning, I'm exhausted, like I told you, just dragging myself around. \n\nBut as far as interfering with my daily life... it's tricky. I mean, I'm still getting to my shipping and receiving shifts on time. I clock in, I check the manifests, I deal with the drivers, I count the pallets. I'm doing my job, you know? I haven't missed a day of work because of this. I'm still providing for my family, still paying the bills. On paper, if you just looked at my schedule, you'd think everything was perfectly fine. Santiago goes to work, Santiago comes home. \n\nBut... but it is changing things at home, in ways that really bother me. See, my wife and kids, sometimes they'll be up a little later, maybe watching a movie in the living room or sitting around the kitchen island having a snack, just talking and laughing. And I used to love that. I used to love just sitting there with them, hearing about their day. But lately... lately I've started avoiding that late family time, especially if they're in or near the kitchen. \n\nI'll just make an excuse, say I'm tired from the distribution center—which isn't a total lie, I am tired—and I'll just head to the bedroom or go sit out in the garage. And the real reason I do it is because I'm just so worried they're going to notice how uneasy I am around the food. If there's a bowl of chips out, or if I see those leftover containers in the fridge when someone opens it to get a drink... I get this anxious, tight feeling. I don't want them to see me staring at it, or see me looking nervous, or ask me why I'm acting weird. So I just remove myself from the situation entirely. I'm missing out on time with my own family in my own house because I'm scared of how I'll act around the food, or what they might see in my face. So yeah, I guess it is interfering, just... just not with my job, but with the stuff that actually matters more.\n\nDoctor: When you're standing in front of the fridge or pantry during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop yourself even though you know you should?\n\nPatient: Well, doc, that's exactly it. That's the scariest part of the whole thing, to be honest with you. It's not just about eating the leftovers, it's about... it's about what happens in my head while I'm doing it. \n\nI'll be standing there, right? The fridge door is open, the little light is on, casting that glow on the kitchen floor. I've got a container of enchiladas open, maybe just a fork, maybe I'm just using my fingers if I'm being completely honest, which I hate admitting. And I'll take a bite, and it tastes good, sure. But then, almost immediately, this voice in my head—my own voice, you know?—says, \"Okay, Santiago, that's enough. You had a taste. Put the lid back on. Close the fridge. Go back to your chair or go to bed.\" I hear it loud and clear. I know I should stop. I know exactly how I'm going to feel the next morning at the distribution center, dragging my feet, feeling that heavy fog while I'm trying to check the invoices. \n\nBut... but it's like my body doesn't listen to my brain anymore. It's the strangest, most frustrating feeling. I'll be telling myself to stop, practically begging myself to just put the plastic lid back on the Tupperware, but my hand... my hand just keeps going back down into the container. It's like I don't even get to make the decision anymore. It's like my arm has a mind of its own, just scoop, lift, chew, swallow. Scoop, lift, chew, swallow. Over and over. \n\nAnd I'm just standing there, watching myself do it, feeling so helpless. I'll think, \"Just step back, Santiago, just take one step back,\" but my feet stay planted right there on the linoleum. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body until the container is empty, or until I'm so uncomfortably full that I physically can't take another bite. That loss of control, doc, it's... it's really messing with my head. Because I'm a guy who likes to be in control. I manage inventory, I keep things organized, I make sure the trucks are loaded right. But standing in front of my own refrigerator at midnight? I have absolutely zero control. It just takes over completely.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, would you say that you feel completely unable to control how much you eat once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, doc. Like I was just trying to say a second ago, that's the whole thing in a nutshell. It's a complete and total lack of control. \n\nI mean, it's not just a little slip-up where you eat an extra cookie and think, \"Oh well, I shouldn't have done that.\" It's... it's like a complete hijacking of my own body. I'll be standing there, right? And I know I shouldn't be eating that fourth enchilada. I'm already full. My stomach is physically tight, practically telling me, \"Hey, buddy, we're good here, we had dinner hours ago.\" And I'm a logical guy, you know? At the warehouse, if a pallet is full, you don't keep stacking boxes on it. It'll tip over, it's a safety hazard, you just stop and get a new pallet. It's basic common sense. \n\nBut with this... what really worries me is that once I start picking at the food late at night, I literally tell myself, \"That's enough, Santiago.\" I say it in my head, sometimes I even whisper it out loud to the open fridge. But my hand just keeps going back, like I don't get to make the decision anymore. \n\nIt’s like the signal from my brain to my arm just gets cut off, like a frayed wire on one of our forklifts. I just stand there, chewing, feeling so pathetic about the whole thing. And then, while I'm still eating, I start stressing about the next day—how I'm going to feel bloated and exhausted, and how I'm gonna have to bury the empty Tupperware at the very bottom of the kitchen trash bin so my wife doesn't see it when she takes the bag out. Because she's so proud of her cooking, right? If she saw I ate three days' worth of her meal prep in twenty minutes... man, it would break her heart, or she'd just think I've lost my mind. So I'm standing there, stressing about the trash, stressing about my stomach, practically begging myself to stop, but the hand just keeps scooping. It’s completely out of my control. I just can't stop the train once it leaves the station.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more rapidly than normal?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah. Yeah, I do. It's actually... it's completely different from how I normally eat. \n\nWhen we sit down for dinner, the whole family, you know, we really take our time. We sit around the table or the kitchen island, we pass the dishes around, we talk about the kids' day at school, I tell them about what happened down at the distribution center. I savor the meal. My wife, she puts a lot of love into her cooking, and I always make sure to enjoy it, to really taste the spices and everything. \n\nBut at one in the morning, standing there in the dark? Man, it's a completely different story. I'm eating so fast, doc. It's nothing at all like when I'm sitting with the family. It's almost like a race against the clock. I'm just shoveling it in, trying to get it all done and over with before anyone wakes up. I'm constantly listening for the floorboards upstairs to creak, terrified my wife is going to get up to use the bathroom or get a glass of water and catch me standing there with my hand in the Tupperware. So I just pack it away as quickly as humanly possible. \n\nAnd because I'm going at that speed, I'm barely even tasting the food. I mean, I know what I'm eating, obviously, but I'm not enjoying it. I'm not savoring the sauce or the cheese or anything. It's just going down the hatch. It reminds me of when we have a rush order at the loading dock, and the trucks are lined up all the way out to the street. You're not looking at the labels on the boxes anymore, you're not organizing them neatly, you're just throwing them onto the pallets as fast as you can to clear the backlog. That's exactly how I'm eating. Just throwing it in, barely chewing. \n\nSometimes I'll even get this sharp pain in my chest or my stomach just from how fast it's all hitting my system, probably swallowing a whole bunch of air along with it. And that just makes the guilt so much worse, you know? Because I'm standing there thinking, what is even the point of eating all my wife's wonderful leftovers if I'm not even taking the time to taste them? I'm just inhaling it in the dark like a thief in my own kitchen, rushing through it so I can hide the evidence before the sun comes up. It's just... it's exhausting, honestly, doing it like that.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself continuing to eat even after you feel full, to the point where you feel physically uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That's exactly how it ends every single time, doc. It goes right back to what I was saying before about the control thing, blowing right past that normal stopping point. I'll feel that signal, you know, the one where any normal person would say, \"Alright, I'm satisfied,\" but I just keep going and going until I'm in actual physical pain. \n\nBy the time I finally force myself to stop—which, honestly, is usually just because the Tupperware is scraped completely clean—my stomach feels so incredibly tight and heavy. It's this awful, stretched-out feeling, kind of like when some of the new guys at the warehouse wrap a pallet way too tight with the industrial shrink wrap and the cardboard boxes start bowing in. That's exactly what my gut feels like. It's just this heavy, solid block sitting right in my middle. \n\nAnd the worst part is what comes after. Because once I've washed the fork and buried the container in the trash, I can't even just go back upstairs and go to sleep. I'd love to just crawl back under the covers and forget it happened, but I physically can't do it. I end up having to sneak into the living room, in the pitch dark, and I just sit there on the edge of the couch for a good while. I have to sit right on the edge, keeping myself propped up, because if I try to lie down, or even if I just bend over slightly to adjust my slippers or something, it feels so bad. It's just this intense, miserable pressure. Bending over or lying flat just makes everything push up, and it hurts, it really does. \n\nSo I just sit there on the edge of the cushions, staring at the wall, listening to the house settle, just waiting for that heavy, tight feeling to pass enough so I can finally go lie horizontally in my bed without feeling like my stomach is going to split open. It takes a long time, too. It's a pretty pathetic way to spend the middle of the night, let me tell you, just sitting there too full to even lay your head down on a pillow.\n\nDoctor: During these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating even when you're not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s the crazy part about all this. A lot of times I'm not even hungry. I mean, not even a little bit. \n\nLike I was saying earlier about my wife's cooking, we always have a proper, sit-down dinner as a family. Usually around 6:30 or 7:00. And it’s a heavy meal, you know? Meat, potatoes, rice, beans—real solid food that sticks to your ribs. I eat a full portion, sometimes I even go back for a little extra while we're all sitting around talking. By the time I help clear the plates and load the dishwasher, I am completely stuffed. I’ll go sit in my recliner to watch some TV and I’m totally satisfied. The tank is completely full. \n\nSo, you'd think that would be it for the night, right? If you're not hungry, you don't eat. It's like down at the loading dock—if a freight truck is already loaded to maximum weight capacity, you don't try to cram another pallet in there just for the heck of it. There's no need for it, the job is done. But with me... fast forward a few hours, the house is dead quiet, everyone is asleep, and I'm not waking up because my stomach is growling. There's zero physical hunger driving it. \n\nBut I still end up wandering downstairs, padding across the kitchen floor in my socks, and going straight for the handle. A lot of times I'm not even hungry, because I already had that big dinner with everybody, but later I still end up opening the fridge and starting again. I'll just stand there staring into it, bathed in that little yellow light, knowing full well my body doesn't need the food. But I just pull the Tupperware out anyway. It’s like my brain completely ignores the fact that I’m not hungry and just forces me to eat regardless. It really makes no logical sense when you sit down and actually think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes in secret because you're embarrassed about how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, a hundred percent, doc. The embarrassment is... well, it's the whole reason I'm doing it at one in the morning in the dark, right? I mean, if I wasn't ashamed of it, I'd just eat during the day. But I really don't like anyone seeing how much I put away. It's just an unbelievable amount of food, and if anyone actually saw the sheer volume of it, I don't know how I'd even look them in the eye the next morning. \n\nIt's like when we do our quarterly inventory audits at the warehouse and you find a massive discrepancy on the ledger—you just don't want anyone to see it because it looks terrible, it looks totally out of control. That's exactly how I feel about the food. So I wait until my aunt—she lives with us now, she's staying in the downstairs guest room right off the kitchen hallway—and my wife and the rest of the house are completely asleep. I have to wait until I'm absolutely sure they're out cold. I'll literally lay there in bed just listening to the house, checking the clock, waiting for everything to settle down and get dead quiet. \n\nAnd then I creep down there and I keep it quiet in the kitchen. I mean, I'm talking total stealth mode. I know exactly which floorboards squeak, so I step over them. I open the fridge door super slow so the magnetic rubber seal doesn't make that loud popping noise... you know the one I'm talking about? And I don't even turn on the overhead light, just rely on the glow from the fridge. Because if my aunt woke up to get a glass of water, or if my wife came downstairs and saw me just tearing through half a roast or a whole family-sized side dish by myself... I'd be mortified. Just absolutely mortified. I couldn't handle the look on their faces. So yeah, the secrecy is all about the shame of it. I just can't let them see me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes usually happen after you've been feeling particularly stressed, upset, or emotionally down earlier in the day?\n\nPatient: You know, doc, I have actually thought about that myself. You always hear about people doing that, right? Like eating their feelings or whatever they call it. But honestly, no. I really haven't noticed any kind of connection like that at all. It doesn't seem to matter if I am stressed or happy or just... neutral. \n\nTake my job, for instance. Shipping and receiving can be an absolute madhouse sometimes. Especially around the end of the month when we are trying to push all the outgoing freight out the door to make the numbers look good for corporate. Just last Thursday we had three delivery trucks show up at the exact same time, all of them needing to be unloaded right that second, and the battery died on our main forklift. I was running around with the manual pallet jack, sweating, frustrated, arguing with the drivers... I came home completely wound up, just exhausted and annoyed. But that night? Nothing. I slept like a rock. I didn't even think about going to the kitchen. \n\nBut then, let us look at a Sunday. A totally normal, lazy Sunday. I am talking about just sitting in the yard, maybe doing a little weeding, watching a ballgame on the TV. Zero stress. Not a care in the world. I am feeling perfectly fine, in a great mood, having a laugh with my wife over dinner. We go to sleep, and sure enough, one thirty in the morning rolls around, and I am sneaking down the stairs doing exactly what I told you about, eating until my stomach hurts. So it really does not line up with being upset or down in the dumps. \n\nIt is frustrating, honestly, because if it was just stress, then maybe I could figure it out. I could tell myself that I had a rough shift at the warehouse and I need to be careful tonight. I could mentally prepare for it. Or I could take up jogging or something to burn off the stress. But because it just happens whenever, on good days, bad days, boring days, it feels even more out of nowhere. I have tried to track it mentally. I will ask myself if I was mad that day, or if I was sad, and the answer is almost always no. I am just a regular guy going about my week. So yeah, the emotional thing, I really do not think that is what is driving the bus here. It is just its own separate thing, completely disconnected from whatever happened during the hours I was awake.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
